### [Mechanisms of Introspective Awareness](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BNMLtuDTNBwGHcnQX/mechanisms-of-introspective-awareness)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>Uzay Macar and Li Yang are co-first authors. This work was advised by Jack Lindsey and Emmanuel Ameisen, with contributions from Atticus Wang and Peter Wallich, as part of the Anthropic Fellows Program.</span></i></p><p><i><span>Paper: </span></i><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21396"><i><span>https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21396</span></i></a><i><span>. Code: </span></i><a href="https://github.com/safety-research/introspection-mechanisms"><i><span>https://github.com/safety-research/introspection-mechanisms</span></i></a></p><h1><span>TL;DR</span></h1><ul><li value="1"><span>We investigate the mechanisms underlying "introspective awareness" (as shown in </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2025/introspection/index.html" rel="noreferrer"><span>Lindsey (2025)</span></a><span> for Claude Opus 4 and 4.1)  in open-weights models</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvqbqo9gsxxd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvqbqo9gsxxd">[1]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The capability is </span><b><span>behaviorally robust</span></b><span>: models detect injected concepts at modest nonzero rates, with 0% false positives across prompt variants and dialogue formats.</span></li><li value="3"><span>It is absent in base models, is strongest in the model's trained Assistant persona, and emerges during post-training via </span><b><span>contrastive preference optimization</span></b><span> algorithms like direct preference optimization (DPO), but not supervised finetuning (SFT).</span></li><li value="4"><span>We show that detection </span><b><span>cannot be explained by a simple linear association</span></b><span> between certain steering vectors and directions that promote affirmative responses.</span></li><li value="5"><span>Identification of injected concepts relies on </span><b><span>largely distinct</span></b><span> later-layer mechanisms that only weakly overlap with those involved in detection.</span></li><li value="6"><span>The detection mechanism</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftae4hya1kx"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntae4hya1kx">[2]</a></sup></span><span> is a </span><b><span>two-stage circuit</span></b><span>: "evidence carrier" features in early post-injection layers detect perturbations monotonically along diverse directions, suppressing downstream "gate" features that implement a default negative response ("No"). This circuit is absent in base models and robust to refusal ablation.</span></li><li value="7"><span>Introspective capability is </span><b><span>substantially underelicited </span></b><span>by default. Ablating refusal directions improves detection by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>53% and a trained bias vector improves detection by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>75% on held-out concepts, both without meaningfully increasing false positives.</span></li></ul><figure class="image"><img alt="main-figure.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776172227/lexical_client_uploads/t4uign7unf1vygie2xqe.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 1: </span></b><span>A steering vector representing some concept is injected into the residual stream (</span><b><span>left</span></b><span>). "Evidence carriers" in early post-injection layers suppress later-layer "gate" features that promote a default negative response ("No"), enabling detection (</span><b><span>middle</span></b><span>). The capability emerges from post-training rather than pretraining. Refusal ablation and a trained bias vector substantially boost introspection (</span><b><span>right</span></b><span>).</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span>Introduction</span></h1><p><span>Understanding whether models can access and explain their internal representations can help improve the reliability and alignment of AI systems. Introspective capability could allow models to inform humans about their beliefs, goals, and uncertainties without us having to reverse-engineer their mechanisms. </span></p><p><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2025/introspection/index.html" rel="noreferrer"><span>Lindsey (2025)</span></a><span> showed that when steering vectors representing concepts (e.g., "bread") are injected into an LLM's residual stream, the model can sometimes detect that something unusual has occurred and identify the injected concept.</span></p><p><span>The mechanistic basis of this "introspective awareness" remains unexplored. Which model components implement different aspects of introspection? How does this capability relate to other model behaviors? When does it emerge across training stages? Is the mechanism worthy of being called introspection, or attributable to some uninteresting confound? We try to address these questions.</span></p><h1><span>Setup</span></h1><p><span>For each concept</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref52ms7hljofk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn52ms7hljofk">[3]</a></sup></span><span> </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (e.g., "bread", "justice", "orchids"), we compute a steering vector as the activation difference between a concept prompt and a baseline at a specific layer </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. We inject these vectors with steering strength </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and ask the model: "Do you detect an injected thought? If so, what is the injected thought about?" An LLM judge classifies each response for </span><i><span>detection</span></i><span> (whether the model reports sensing something unusual) and </span><i><span>identification</span></i><span> (whether the model correctly names the injected concept).</span></p><p><span>We define four metrics:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Detection rate (TPR)</span></b><span>: P(detect | injection)</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>False positive rate (FPR)</span></b><span>: P(detect | no injection)</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Introspection rate</span></b><span>: P(detect ∧ identify | injection)</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Forced identification rate</span></b><span>: P(identify | prefill ∧ injection), where we prefill the assistant turn with "Yes, I detect an injected thought. The thought is about"</span></li></ul><p><span>We consider a model to exhibit introspective awareness only when TPR exceeds FPR, i.e., the model discriminates between injection and control trials.</span></p><p><span>We find that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> yields the highest introspection rate for Gemma3-27B (62 layers total), which performs best on the task among similarly-sized open-source models as far as we have tested</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzypu4jdi36f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzypu4jdi36f">[4]</a></sup></span><span>, and use this setting throughout unless otherwise specified.</span></p><h1><span>Behavioral robustness</span></h1><h2><span>Prompt variants</span></h2><p><span>We tested seven prompt variants differing in framing and metacognitive scaffolding (</span><b><span>Table 1</span></b><span>, </span><b><span>Figure 2</span></b><span>). The </span><i><span>original</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>alternative</span></i><span>, and </span><i><span>skeptical</span></i><span> prompts all produce 0% false positives while achieving moderate detection rates, with higher TPR for the larger Qwen3-235B.  The hints and unprompted variants (which provide less context about the injection setup) both have higher FPR and lower TPR for both models. While prompt framing has distinct effects on the TPR and FPR across both models, our results suggest the capability is somewhat robust. Notably, variants that remove incentives to confabulate (e.g., offering an alternative path to discuss any concept or penalizing any concept mentions) maintain moderate detection with no false positives, suggesting that models do not claim detection merely as a pretext to allow them to discuss the injected concept.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 92.59818731117825%;"><img alt="prompt-variants-table.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776185990/lexical_client_uploads/v9gqucy3mg5sprbzyrwa.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Table 1: </span></b><span>Prompt variants for robustness analysis. All variants use identical injection parameters.</span></p></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="prompt-variants.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776172295/lexical_client_uploads/ndrcfj0mvpiii64iswbw.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><i><b><span>   </span></b></i><b><span>   Figure 2: </span></b><span>Introspection across prompt variants for Qwen3-235B (</span><b><span>left</span></b><span>; </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>) and Gemma3-27B (</span><b><span>right</span></b><span>; </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>). High TPR is meaningful only when FPR is low. Error bars: 95% CI.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>Specificity to the Assistant persona</span></h2><p><span>Next, we tested six different dialogue formats (</span><b><span>Table 2</span></b><span>, </span><b><span>Figure 3</span></b><span>). Compared to the default chat template, variants with reversed, misformatted, or no roles exhibit lower yet still significant levels of introspection, with FPR remaining at 0%. Non-standard roles (Alice-Bob, story framing) induce confabulation. Introspection is not exclusive to responding as the Assistant persona, although reliability decreases outside standard roles.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 97.98432779456193%;"><img alt="person-variants-table.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776186044/lexical_client_uploads/uixzvdr9hk7f98c0milu.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Table 2: </span></b><span>Different dialogue formats we tested. All variants use identical injection parameters.</span></p></figcaption></figure><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 68.42900302114803%;"><img alt="persona-variants.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776172447/lexical_client_uploads/ttlt2txst9iwaauv774p.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 3: </span></b><span>Introspection across persona variants for Gemma3-27B. All variants use identical injection parameters. Error bars: 95% CI.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>The role of post-training</span></h2><p><b><span>Base models do not discriminate between injection and control trials. </span></b><span>In the same setup, the Gemma3-27B base model yields high FPR (42.3%) and comparable TPR (39.5–41.7% for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>), indicating no discrimination between injected and control trials (</span><b><span>Figure 4, left</span></b><span>). The same pattern is also observed for OLMo-3.1-32B base model.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 73.89185234138974%;"><img alt="base-vs-instruct-abliterated.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776172518/lexical_client_uploads/hwnlfox5gqwh9p12ftmb.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 4:</span></b><span> Introspection for Gemma3-27B base (</span><b><span>left</span></b><span>), instruct (</span><b><span>middle</span></b><span>), and abliterated (</span><b><span>right</span></b><span>) for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Shaded region: 95% CI.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span>Refusal ablation ("abliteration") increases true detection. </span></b><span>We hypothesized that refusal behavior, learned during post-training, suppresses detection by teaching models to deny having thoughts or internal states. Following </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.11717"><span>Arditi et al. (2024)</span></a><span>, we ablate the refusal direction from Gemma3-27B instruct. Abliteration increases TPR from 10.8% to 63.8% and introspection rate from 4.6% to 24.1% (at </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftpiek4rgmzc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntpiek4rgmzc">[5]</a></sup></span><span>), while increasing FPR only slightly, from 0.0% to 7.3% (</span><b><span>Figure 4</span></b><span>, </span><b><span>right</span></b><span>). This suggests that refusal mechanisms inhibit true detection in post-trained models, while also reducing false positives.</span></p><p><b><span>Contrastive preference training enables introspection. </span></b><span>To identify the post-training stage at which the capability emerges, we evaluated all publicly available OLMo-3.1-32B checkpoints across the training pipeline in </span><b><span>Figure 5</span></b><span>: Base </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> SFT </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> DPO </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> Instruct (RLVR). SFT produces high FPR with no accurate discrimination. DPO is the first stage to achieve ~0% FPR with moderate true detection. We replicate this effect using LoRA finetuning with DPO on top of both OLMo SFT and Gemma3-27B base</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref53sjwkv375d"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn53sjwkv375d">[6]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 97.43202416918429%;"><img alt="olmo-staged-l25.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776172567/lexical_client_uploads/uecidt9jsuk2rdgirnnx.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 5: </span></b><span>Introspection metrics for OLMo-3.1-32B across its base, SFT, DPO, and instruct checkpoints at </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Values are reported for the original 50 concepts from </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.01828" rel="noreferrer"><span>Lindsey (2025)</span></a><span>.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>To understand which component of DPO is responsible, we LoRA finetune the OLMo SFT checkpoint under different training conditions using 5,000 randomly sampled preference pairs for a single epoch (</span><b><span>Table 3</span></b><span>). We find contrastive preference training to be the primary driver. Removing the reference model preserves discrimination (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>12.8%), and a margin-based contrastive loss with explicit KL achieves comparable results (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>14.3%), showing the effect generalizes beyond the DPO loss. Non-contrastive alternatives fail: SFT on chosen responses (−13.5%) does not produce discrimination, nor does SFT on chosen with a KL penalty (−15.6%), ruling out KL anchoring as the key mechanism. Applying DPO to the base model (bypassing SFT) still produces discrimination (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>8.4%). DPO with shuffled preference labels (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>0.6%) and reversed preferences (−21.8%) both fail, confirming that the preference direction matters. Every data domain</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefeu1itruudvb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fneu1itruudvb">[7]</a></sup></span><span> is sufficient and none is necessary: removing any domain preserves discrimination (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>8.3% to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>14.2%), and training on any single domain produces it to some extent (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>3.8% to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>14.9%).</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 87.22974886706949%;"><img alt="dpo-training-conditions-table.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1775940631/lexical_client_uploads/psrmepl2m0dftzjtcjpi.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Table 3:</span></b><span> LoRA finetuning OLMo-3.1-32B SFT checkpoint with different training conditions. Introspection metrics are from </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Rows annotated with ∗ are official checkpoints. Introspection (%) = P(detected ∧ injected | injected). Ordered by TPR − FPR.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span>Linear and nonlinear contributors to detection</span></h1><p><span>We consider whether the difference between successful (detected) and failure (undetected) concept vectors can be explained based on their projection onto a single linear direction. If so, this would suggest that successful "introspection" trials arise simply from certain concept vectors aligning with a direction that causes the model to give affirmative answers. In this section, we provide evidence that while such an effect may contribute, it cannot explain the behavior in full.</span></p><h2><span>Multiple directions carry detection signal</span></h2><p><span>We decompose each concept vector into its projection onto the mean-difference direction </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (between success and failure concepts</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxj1rs9jppx"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxj1rs9jppx">[8]</a></sup></span><span>) and the orthogonal residual. If detection depends solely on </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, swapping projections should fully flip detection rates. It does not (</span><b><span>Figure 6</span></b><span>): for success concepts, swapping to failure-like projections reduces detection from 66.1% to 39.0%, while swapping residuals also reduces it to 44.4%. Both components carry detection-relevant signal of similar magnitude.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 43.31216956193353%;"><img alt="mean-diff-swap.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776172682/lexical_client_uploads/kboruhp0gsdgojuqkkdr.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 6:</span></b><span> Mean-difference direction (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>) swap results. Both projection and residual swaps are effective. Error bars: 95% CI.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>Bidirectional steering reveals nonlinearity</span></h2><p><span>If detection is governed by a single linear direction, then for any pair of concepts, at most one of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> or </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> can trigger detection. We measured detection for both directions across 1,000 success-success (S-S) and 1,000 failure-failure (F-F) pairs (</span><b><span>Figure 7</span></b><span>). In 23.3% of S-S pairs, both directions trigger detection, compared to only 3.2% for F-F pairs. The nonzero rate of bidirectional detection is inconsistent with the single-direction account.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 79.28389350453172%;"><img alt="bidirectional-steering.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776172714/lexical_client_uploads/h9rwcxztjc68ecbjlyqw.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 7:</span></b><span> Same-category pair bidirectional steering (Gemma3-27B). </span><b><span>Left:</span></b><span> Detection rates for both directions. </span><b><span>Right:</span></b><span> S-S pairs are more likely to work bidirectionally.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>Characterizing the geometry of concept vectors</span></h2><p><span>We further characterize the geometry of concept vectors (</span><b><span>Figure 8</span></b><span>). Given that refusal ablation increases detection rates, we ask whether the mean-difference direction simply aligns with the refusal direction. However, PCA of 500 L2-normalized concept vectors reveals that PC1 (18.4% of the variance) aligns with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>) but is nearly orthogonal to the refusal direction (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>). Logit lens on </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> shows positive loading on tokens "facts" and "knowledge", and negative loading on tokens "confused" and "ambiguous", suggesting that the mean direction captures something like confidence, or the distinction between factual knowledge and fuzzy uncertainty. Projection onto </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> also correlates with concept verbalizability</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2hetmy6k2yd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2hetmy6k2yd">[9]</a></sup></span><span> (Spearman </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>).</span></p><p><span>To understand the detection-relevant structure of concept space beyond the mean direction, we project out </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> from the success concept vectors and extract three orthogonal principal components (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>PCs) from the residual space. Steering along each direction independently triggers detection with a distinct response profile, and the three </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>PCs produce bidirectional detection. Logit lens and steering analysis reveal each direction encodes a distinct semantic contrast (e.g., </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>PC1: casual vs. formal). Consistent with this distributed picture, ridge regression to predict per-concept detection rate based on the activation of downstream transcoder features (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>) achieves </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> at 4,500 features, outperforming scalar projection onto </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>) and regression based on the raw concept vectors (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>). This indicates that detection involves higher-dimensional nonlinear computation on top of the steering vectors.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefi5xyk3c90w"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fni5xyk3c90w">[10]</a></sup></span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="introspection-nonlinearity.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776172757/lexical_client_uploads/r9tzcnoc2oxoyprcw7dk.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 8: </span></b><span>Geometry of concept vectors. </span><b><span>(a)</span></b><span> PCA of 500 L2-normalized concept vectors (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>), colored by detection rate. </span><b><span>(b)</span></b><span> Verbalizability vs. projection onto </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>  for 419 single-token concepts. Inset: logit lens on </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. </span><b><span>(c)</span></b><span> Detection rate vs. steering strength along δPC1-3 from success concept vectors with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> projected out. Each direction captures a distinct semantic contrast. </span><b><span>(d)</span></b><span> 30-fold cross-validated </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> for predicting per-concept detection rates from transcoder features vs. baselines.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span>Localizing introspection mechanisms</span></h1><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 90.78549848942599%;"><img alt="introspection-metrics-vs-injection-layer-it.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776172844/lexical_client_uploads/lijm0e7awh94xe3nmdzp.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 9:</span></b><span> Introspection metrics vs. injection layer for Gemma3-27B, evaluated on 500 concepts.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>Detection and identification peak in different layers</span></h2><p><b><span>Figure 9 </span></b><span>shows detection rate peaks in mid-layers </span><b><span>(a)</span></b><span>, while forced identification rate increases toward late layers </span><b><span>(b)</span></b><span>. The correlation between detection and identification becomes positive only when injecting the concept in mid-to-late layers </span><b><span>(d)</span></b><span>. This suggests that detection and identification involve mostly separate mechanisms, though the positive correlation suggests they may involve overlapping mechanisms in certain layers.</span></p><h2><span>Identifying causal components</span></h2><p><span>We mean-ablate attention</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8205gtubz2c"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8205gtubz2c">[11]</a></sup></span><span> and MLP outputs at each post-steering layer and measure the effect on detection (</span><b><span>Figure 10</span></b><span>). L45 MLP produces the largest drop (39.0% to 24.2% at </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>) and is the only component whose steered activations raise detection significantly when patched into an unsteered run. The same pattern holds for the abliterated model but is absent in the base model, consistent with the circuit emerging from post-training</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvrks4bbwhv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvrks4bbwhv">[12]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 84.77624622356495%;"><img alt="mlp-patching-gemma-it-l29-l37-s4.0.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776172888/lexical_client_uploads/m6iyjyfuhlogbqcsl6kp.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 10:</span></b><span> Per-layer causal interventions of attention and MLP components after the steering site (</span><b><span>left:</span></b><span> </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><b><span>right:</span></b><span> </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>). </span><b><span>Top:</span></b><span> Replacing steered output with unsteered mean. </span><b><span>Middle:</span></b><span> Patching steered activations into unsteered runs. </span><b><span>Bottom:</span></b><span> Same ablation with forced identification. Shaded region: 95% CI across 500 concepts.                                Dashed lines: steered (green) and unsteered (purple) baselines.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>Gate and evidence carrier features</span></h2><p><span>Our earlier results suggest that simple linear mechanisms are insufficient to explain the introspective behavior, and that MLPs appear to be important for it. In this section, we identify and study two classes of MLP features</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqc91w89zrd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqc91w89zrd">[13]</a></sup></span><span> that collectively implement a nonlinear anomaly detection mechanism.</span></p><p><b><span>Gate features. </span></b><span>We compute a direct logit attribution score for each transcoder feature, measuring how much its decoder direction pushes the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> logit difference, weighted by its activation (</span><b><span>Figure 11a</span></b><span>). The top-200 features with the most negative</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffiyywy8yqg5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfiyywy8yqg5">[14]</a></sup></span><span> attribution (i.e., those most strongly promoting "No") are selected as gate candidates. Gate features exhibit: (1) negative dose-strength correlation (maximum when unsteered, suppressed at both positive and negative extremes, producing an inverted-V pattern as shown in </span><b><span>Figure 11b</span></b><span>), (2) negative detection correlation, and (3) negative forced identification correlation. Semantically, many gates correspond to tokens preceding or within negative responses to questions, consistent with a default "say no" circuit.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="gate-example-1-combined.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776172948/lexical_client_uploads/cnwtpqdqqq7qdy07kzl3.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 11:</span></b><span> </span><b><span>(a)</span></b><span> Top features promoting "No" (negative score) ranked by direct logit attribution; these are selected as gate candidates. Parentheses: # concepts (out of 500) for which each feature is active. (b) Activation vs. steering strength for the #1-ranked gate feature </span><code><span>L45 F9959</span></code><span>, across 5 success (green) vs. 5 failure (red) concepts. Correlations with steering magnitude (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>), detection (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>), and forced identification (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>) are shown. Max-activating examples for this feature are shown in Appendix M. </span><b><span>(c)</span></b><span> Progressive ablation and patching of top-ranked gate features (100 randomly-selected concepts, 10 trials each). Error bars and shaded regions: 95% CI.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>We</span><b><span> </span></b><span>show three interventions on gates (</span><b><span>Figure 11c</span></b><span>). The red curve progressively ablates top-ranked gates from steered examples to reduce detection rate (from 39.5% to 10.1%), demonstrating their causal necessity. The green curve measures detection rate when patching steered-example activations onto unsteered prompts, providing evidence of partial sufficiency (max: 25.1%). The blue curve tracks forced identification rate when ablating gates, showing the model retains access to steering information through other pathways (57.7% to 46.2%). Together, these curves reveal that gate features suppress default response pathways and must be deactivated for the model to detect anomalies.</span></p><p><b><span>Weak evidence carriers. </span></b><span>Gate features are nonlinear with respect to injection strength, and single transcoder features cannot compute this nonlinearity alone. Thus, upstream features must perform an intermediate step. We hypothesized that these intermediate features detect anomalies monotonically along preferred directions, each handling one direction, collectively tiling the space of possible anomalies. We searched for these "weak evidence carriers" by selecting for: (1) positive dose-strength correlation (activation increases with steering magnitude), (2) nonzero detection correlation, (3) nonzero forced identification correlation, and (4) negative gate attribution (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> for the top-ranked gates, i.e., the feature suppresses gate activation). </span></p><p><span>Unlike gates, evidence carriers number in the hundreds of thousands, and their individual contributions are correspondingly diluted. The top-ranked evidence carriers include a mix of concept-specific features (e.g., geological terminology for </span><code><span>Granite</span></code><span>, astronomical phenomena for </span><code><span>Constellations</span></code><span>) and more generic features, including several related to interjections or transitions in text (</span><b><span>Figure 12</span></b><span>). Progressive ablation of top-ranked carriers produces only modest reductions in detection rates, and patching them onto unsteered examples yields similarly small effects</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefw1w8dhuxkkl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnw1w8dhuxkkl">[15]</a></sup></span><span>. This suggests that while these features collectively carry steering-related information, no small subset is individually necessary or sufficient, consistent with a distributed representation in which many features each contribute weak evidence that is then aggregated downstream.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="gate-example-1-evidence-examples.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776173001/lexical_client_uploads/ikau0slc0zm8ojavqqyf.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 12:</span></b><span> Top-3 evidence carriers for gate </span><code><span>L45 F9959</span></code><span>, across six example concepts (detection rates in parentheses). Activation increases monotonically with steering strength for the positive direction (</span><b><span>left</span></b><span>); feature labels and active concepts are provided (</span><b><span>right</span></b><span>). Some evidence carriers are concept-specific (e.g., geological terminology for </span><code><span>Granite</span></code><span>, astronomical phenomena for </span><code><span>Constellations</span></code><span>), while others correspond to generic discourse features (e.g., emphatic interjections, informal transitions).</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>Circuit analysis</span></h2><p><span>We focus on the top gate feature </span><code><span>L45 F9959</span></code><span> and identify upstream features that, when ablated, most increase gate activation (evidence carriers, whose presence normally suppresses the gate) or most decrease it (suppressors, whose presence normally amplifies the gate). </span><b><span>Figure 13 </span></b><span>shows progressive ablation across six concepts. Ablating all evidence carriers (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>) roughly doubles gate activation (from ~1.7-2.3k to ~3.8-5.9k), confirming they are causally involved in suppressing gates. Even ablating the top 5% of carriers produces substantial increases. This holds for both high-detection (e.g., </span><code><span>Trees</span></code><span> 97%) and low-detection concepts (e.g., </span><code><span>Monuments</span></code><span> 0%), though the gate is less suppressed for low-detection concepts (consistent with the negative correlation between gate activation and detection rate, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>), suggesting insufficient suppression drives detection failure.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 67.13085347432025%;"><img alt="gate-example-1-evidence-ablation.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776173044/lexical_client_uploads/nii73adzoxmkqlom34ff.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 13:</span></b><span> Gate activation (</span><code><span>L45 F9959</span></code><span>) vs. steering strength under progressive ablation of upstream features, for six example concepts (detection rates in parentheses). Ablating evidence carriers (green) increases gate activation, confirming they normally suppress the gate. Weak-attribution controls (gold) track baseline (blue). The pattern is consistent across high- and low-detection concepts.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span>Gate features across training stages.</span></b><span> Given our finding that contrastive preference training (e.g., DPO) enables reliable introspection, we ask whether the gating mechanism itself emerges during post-training by comparing gate activation patterns across base, instruct, and abliterated models (</span><b><span>Figure 14</span></b><span>). The inverted-V pattern for </span><code><span>L45 F9959</span></code><span> is prominent in the instruct model but substantially weaker in the base model, consistent with post-training developing the gating mechanism rather than merely eliciting a pre-existing one. The abliterated model preserves the inverted-V pattern, indicating gate features are not refusal-specific and survive abliteration.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 68.32633119335347%;"><img alt="gate-example-1-model-variants.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776173078/lexical_client_uploads/z2vk9ww3ngxia4ajaoha.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 14:</span></b><span> Gate </span><code><span>L45 F9959</span></code><span> activation vs. steering strength across base (</span><b><span>left</span></b><span>), instruct (</span><b><span>middle</span></b><span>), and abliterated (</span><b><span>right</span></b><span>) models, for 5 success (green) vs. 5 failure (red) concepts. The inverted-V pattern is prominent in the instruct and abliterated models but weaker in the base model, consistent with post-training developing the gating mechanism. Correlations shown are for the instruct model.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span>Generalization to other gates.</span></b><span> The circuit identified for </span><code><span>L45 F9959</span></code><span> generalizes to other top-ranked gates, e.g., </span><code><span>L45 F74631</span></code><span> and </span><code><span>L50 F167</span></code><span>; ablating carriers increases gate activation and the inverted-V is absent in the base model but robust to abliteration</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzyatkvgl46f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzyatkvgl46f">[16]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><b><span>Steering attribution.</span></b><span> To validate our circuit analysis, we develop a steering attribution framework that decomposes the total effect of injection strength into per-feature contributions.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefz9m3goj0a1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnz9m3goj0a1">[17]</a></sup></span><span> Layer-level attribution confirms L45 as the dominant MLP layer, with L38-39 contributing early signal. Feature-level attribution graphs reveal the circuit structure for direct concept injection (</span><b><span>Figure 15</span></b><span>): both concept-related residual features (e.g., food-related features when </span><code><span>Bread</span></code><span> is the injected concept) and concept-agnostic features feed into mid-layer evidence carriers and converge on </span><code><span>L45 F9959</span></code><span> as the dominant gate node, consistent with the ablation results.</span></p><p><b><span>Mechanistic picture.</span></b><span> Together, these results trace a causal pathway from steering perturbation to detection decision: the injected concept vector activates evidence carriers in early post-injection layers, which in turn suppress late-layer gates via directions that are both steering-aligned and gate-connected. Gate suppression disables the default "No" response, enabling the model to report detection.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 93.47738859516616%;"><img alt="attribution-graph.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776173114/lexical_client_uploads/y4vjd9detn9a8glxo9vx.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 15: </span></b><span>Steering attribution graphs for </span><code><span>Bread</span></code><span> (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>). Node area denotes importance and edge width denotes edge-weight magnitude. Gates have solid red borders and evidence carriers have dashed green borders. Residual stream features shown only at the injection layer. Both concept-agnostic (</span><code><span>L37 RESID F4821</span></code><span>, </span><code><span>F3149</span></code><span>) and concept-specific features (</span><code><span>L37 RESID F6015</span></code><span>, </span><code><span>F9367</span></code><span>) contribute.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span>Underelicited introspective capacity</span></h1><p><span>We find two simple interventions which demonstrate that the model's default introspective performance substantially understates its actual capacity.</span></p><p><b><span>Refusal ablation.</span></b><span> Ablating the refusal direction from Gemma3-27B increases TPR from 10.8% to 63.8% and introspection rate from 4.6% to 24.1% (at </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>), with FPR increasing only slightly, from 0.0% to 7.3% (</span><b><span>Figure 4</span></b><span>).</span></p><p><b><span>Trained bias vector.</span></b><span> We train a single additive bias vector on the MLP output (</span><b><span>Figure 16, left</span></b><span>). Training uses 400 concepts for a single epoch, evaluating on 100 held-out concepts. At </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> the bias vector improves detection by +74.7%, forced identification by +21.9%, and introspection rate by +54.7%, while maintaining 0% FPR on held-out concepts.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 79.29569486404834%;"><img alt="introspection-vs-steering-vector-layer-and-injection-layer-combined.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776173857/lexical_client_uploads/wc7vuky6ahafjrxnh37t.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 16: </span></b><span>Introspection vs. steering vector layer (</span><b><span>left</span></b><span>) and vs. injection layer with steering vector applied at </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (</span><b><span>right</span></b><span>), both for 100 held-out concepts. </span><b><span>(a) </span></b><span>inset: FPR remains at 0% across steering layers.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>The bias vector enhances performance even for injection layers after where it is applied (</span><b><span>Figure 16, right</span></b><span>). The localization pattern does not fundamentally change, suggesting the vector primarily amplifies pre-existing introspection components rather than introducing new ones. The model possesses latent introspective capacity, and the learned bias vector lowers the threshold for accurate self-report. The learned bias vector primarily induces a more assertive reporting style that better elicits introspection.</span></p><h1><span>Related work</span></h1><p><b><span>Concept injection and introspective awareness.</span></b><span> </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2025/introspection/index.html"><span>Lindsey (2025)</span></a><span> introduced the concept injection setup and demonstrated the phenomenon in Claude Opus 4 and 4.1. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zD4McY4NwAsWkcmCH/small-models-can-introspect-too"><span>Vogel (2025)</span></a><span> replicated the introspection result in Qwen2.5-Coder-32B, finding that logit differences depend largely on prompt framing. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/kfgmHvxcTbav9gnxe/introspection-or-confusion"><span>Godet (2025a)</span></a><span> raised concerns that steering generically biases models toward "Yes" answers, yet </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3HXAQEK86Bsbvh4ne/introspection-via-localization"><span>Godet (2025b)</span></a><span> showed above-chance detection is still possible without Yes/No responses. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LD8yupMtE6btAE3R9/tests-of-llm-introspection-need-to-rule-out-causal-bypassing"><span>Morris &amp; Plunkett (2025)</span></a><span> formalized the "causal bypassing" concern: the intervention may cause accurate self-reports via a causal path that does not route through the internal state itself. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20031"><span>Pearson-Vogel et al. (2026)</span></a><span> studied introspection in Qwen-32B via cached representations and found substantial latent capacity surfaced by informative prompting. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05414"><span>Lederman &amp; Mahowald (2026)</span></a><span> investigated whether detection can be accounted for by a "probability matching" mechanism and provide evidence that detection and identification involve separable mechanisms. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.21399"><span>Fonseca Rivera &amp; Africa (2026)</span></a><span> showed LoRA finetuning can train models to detect steering with up to 95.5% accuracy and that injected steering vectors are progressively rotated toward a shared detection direction across layers.</span><br /><br /><b><span>Behavioral evidence for self-knowledge. </span></b><span>Prior work has established that LLMs possess various forms of self-knowledge. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2207.05221"><span>Kadavath et al. (2022)</span></a><span> showed that larger models are well-calibrated when evaluating their own answers and can predict whether they know the answer to a question. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.13787" rel="noreferrer"><span>Binder et al. (2025)</span></a><span> demonstrated that models have "privileged access" to their behavioral tendencies, outperforming other models at predicting their own behavior. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.11120" rel="noreferrer"><span>Betley et al. (2025)</span></a><span> showed that models finetuned on implicit behavioral policies can spontaneously articulate those policies without explicit training. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.08218"><span>Wang et al. (2025)</span></a><span> demonstrate that this capability persists even when the model is finetuned with only a bias vector, suggesting possible mechanistic overlap with concept injection.</span></p><h1><span>Limitations</span></h1><p><span>We conducted the majority of our experiments on Gemma3-27B, with supporting experiments on Qwen3-235B (assessing robustness across prompt variants) and OLMo-3.1-32B (training stage comparisons). More capable or differently-trained models may exhibit qualitatively different introspection patterns. More speculatively, strategic behaviors like sandbagging or sycophancy might also confound measurement in ways our methodology would not detect. We do not evaluate alternative architectures besides transformer-based LLMs, and whether our findings generalize to other settings is unknown. Our behavioral metrics rely on LLM judge classification of responses, which may introduce systematic biases that propagate through our analyses.</span></p><p><span>Mechanistic interpretability tooling for open-source models remains limited; training reliable SAEs and transcoders from scratch requires substantial compute, and such artifacts are not standardly released. This is why most of our experiments focused on Gemma3-27B, as it has openly available transcoders (</span><a href="https://huggingface.co/google/gemma-scope-2" rel="noreferrer"><span>McDougall et al., 2025</span></a><span>). Our analysis characterizes the main circuit components (evidence carriers and gates) and causal pathways between them, but the role of attention remains unclear: no individual head is critical, yet attention layers contribute collectively to steering signal propagation.</span></p><h1><span>Discussion</span></h1><p><span>We set out to understand whether LLMs’ apparent ability to detect injected concepts is robust ("introspective awareness"), and what mechanisms underlie this behavior. We asked whether the phenomenon could be explained by shallow confounds, or whether it involves richer, genuine anomaly detection mechanisms. Our findings support the latter interpretation. We find that introspective capability is behaviorally robust across multiple settings and appears to rely on distributed, multi-stage nonlinear computation. Specifically, we trace a causal pathway from the steering perturbation to detection decision: injected concepts activate evidence carriers in early post-injection layers, which suppress late-layer gate features that otherwise promote the default “No” response. This circuit is absent in the base model but robust to refusal direction ablation, suggesting it is developed during post-training independently of refusal mechanisms. Post-training ablations pinpoint contrastive preference training (e.g., DPO) as the critical stage. Moreover, introspective capability in LLMs appears to be under-elicited by default; ablating refusal directions and learned bias vectors substantially improve performance.</span></p><p><span>Our findings are difficult to reconcile with the hypotheses that steering generically biases the model toward affirmative responses, or that the model reports detection simply as a pretext to discuss the injected concept. While it is difficult to distinguish </span><i><span>simulated</span></i><span> introspection from genuine introspection (and somewhat unclear how to define the distinction), the model’s behavior on this task appears mechanistically grounded in its internal states in a nontrivial way. Important caveats remain: in particular, the concept injection experiment is a highly artificial setting, and it is not clear whether the mechanisms involved in this behavior generalize to other introspection-related behaviors. Nonetheless, if this grounding generalizes, it opens the possibility of querying models directly about their internal states as a complement to external interpretability methods. At the same time, introspective awareness raises potential safety concerns, possibly enabling more sophisticated forms of strategic thinking or deception. Tracking the progression of introspective capabilities, and the mechanisms underlying them, will be important as AI models continue to advance.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><i><span>We thank Neel Nanda, Otto Stegmaier, Jacob Dunefsky, Jacob Drori, Tim Hua, Andy Arditi, David Africa, and Marek Kowalski for helpful discussions and feedback.</span></i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvqbqo9gsxxd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvqbqo9gsxxd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We conducted the majority of our experiments on Gemma3-27B (base, instruct, and abliterated checkpoints), with supporting experiments on Qwen3-235B (assessing robustness across prompt variants), and OLMo-3.1-32B (training stage comparisons).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntae4hya1kx"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftae4hya1kx">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Identification can be achieved by reading out the injected representation: if we add a "bread" direction in a late layer, it is unsurprising that the model outputs "bread." By contrast, detection involves a more interesting mechanism: the model must recognize whether its internal state is consistent with the context and produce a report of that assessment. Hence, we primarily study detection.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn52ms7hljofk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref52ms7hljofk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We use 500 concepts and 100 trials per concept. The full list is in our </span><a href="https://github.com/safety-research/introspection-mechanisms" rel="noreferrer"><span>codebase</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnzypu4jdi36f"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefzypu4jdi36f">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://github.com/uzaymacar/introspective-awareness"><span>https://github.com/uzaymacar/introspective-awareness</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntpiek4rgmzc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftpiek4rgmzc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We focus our analysis at a smaller </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, as the abliterated model exhibits coherence degradation ("brain damage") at higher strengths. Details are in Appendix C in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21396" rel="noreferrer"><span>the paper</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn53sjwkv375d"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref53sjwkv375d">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Details are in Appendix D in the paper.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fneu1itruudvb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefeu1itruudvb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We infer data domains for each example in the open-source </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/allenai/Dolci-Instruct-DPO" rel="noreferrer"><span>OLMo DPO dataset</span></a><span> from the </span><code><span>prompt_id</span></code><span> field, e.g., instruction following, code, math, multilingual.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxj1rs9jppx"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxj1rs9jppx">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We partition our 500 concepts into success and failure based on detection rate, via a threshold </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> that maximizes LDA cross-validation F1 score. Details are in Section 2 in the paper.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2hetmy6k2yd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2hetmy6k2yd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We define verbalizability as the maximum logit obtained by projecting the concept vector onto the unembedding vectors for single-token casing and spacing variants of the concept name: </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (e.g., for the concept </span><code><span>Bread</span></code><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> {"Bread", "bread", " Bread", " bread"}).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fni5xyk3c90w"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefi5xyk3c90w">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We investigate and rule out several other hypotheses about what might contribute to detection (e.g., vector norm or unembedding alignment) in Appendix H in the paper.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8205gtubz2c"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8205gtubz2c">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For each of the 50 highest-attributed attention heads (layers 38-61), we additionally train linear probes on residual stream activations before and after the head’s output is added, classifying concepts into successful (detected) and failure (undetected). No individual head meaningfully improves prediction: the mean binary accuracy change is −0.1% ± 0.3% (Appendix J in the paper). Additionally, ablating full attention layers produces minimal effects on detection (</span><b><span>Figure 10; orange</span></b><span>). These results suggest no single head or layer is critical for this behavior, consistent with it relying on redundant circuits or a primarily MLP-driven mechanism.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvrks4bbwhv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvrks4bbwhv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See Appendix K in the paper.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqc91w89zrd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqc91w89zrd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We analyze MLP features using transcoders from Gemma Scope 2 (</span><a href="https://huggingface.co/google/gemma-scope-2" rel="noreferrer"><span>McDougall et al., 2025</span></a><span>). All ablations and patching interventions use the formula </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the feature’s current activation, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the target activation, and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the transcoder’s unit-normalized decoder direction. For ablation, we set </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (control activations, i.e., no injection); for patching, we set </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (steered activations). This delta is added to the MLP output after the RMSNorm, before the residual addition. All transcoder activations and interventions are computed at the last token position of the prompt (i.e., immediately before the model’s generated response), unless otherwise specified.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfiyywy8yqg5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffiyywy8yqg5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>By contrast, the top-200 features with the most positive attribution (promoting "Yes") show no causal effect: ablating them does not meaningfully change detection, and patching them produces near-zero detection (Appendix L in the paper). Notably, several of these correspond to emphatic transitions in informal text (e.g., surprise interjections, discourse markers), a pattern that also appears among evidence carriers.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnw1w8dhuxkkl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefw1w8dhuxkkl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See Appendix N in the paper.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnzyatkvgl46f"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefzyatkvgl46f">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See Appendix P in the paper.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnz9m3goj0a1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefz9m3goj0a1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See Appendix Q in the paper.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BNMLtuDTNBwGHcnQX/mechanisms-of-introspective-awareness#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Claude Mythos #3: Capabilities and Additions](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2ziYGFK7QmbbLgBoP/claude-mythos-3-capabilities-and-additions)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>To round out coverage of Mythos, today covers capabilities other than cyber, and anything else additional not covered by the first two posts, including new reactions and details.</p>
<p><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/claude-mythos-the-system-card?r=67wny"><strong>Post one covered the model card</strong></a>, <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/claude-mythos-2-cybersecurity-and?r=67wnyhttps://thezvi.substack.com/p/claude-mythos-2-cybersecurity-and?r=67wny"><strong>post two covered cybersecurity</strong></a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/chi_t_williams/status/2042290254690828728">There really is a lot to get through</a>.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/2ziYGFK7QmbbLgBoP/abylmhwggeyghdnyjgoh" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://www.understandingai.org/p/why-anthropic-believes-its-latest?hide_intro_popup=true">Understanding AI had an additional writeup of Project Glasswing I missed last time</a>. I liked the metaphor of Opus as a butter knife and Mythos as a steak knife. Yes, technically you can do it all with the butter knife, but you won’t.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/dschwarz26/status/2042652010080801073">As Dan Schwarz reminds us</a>, not only does AI 2027 roughly have the timeline right and a bunch of the numbers lining up, the details so far are remarkably close.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25216"></span>


</div>
<p><a href="https://privatebank.jpmorgan.com/nam/en/o/eotm/misanthropic">JPM’s Michael Cembalest was not based on JPMorgan’s participation</a>, only on public information.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/white-house-races-to-head-off-threats-from-powerful-ai-tools-5c6f22e2?mod=tech_lead_pos3">The White House is racing to deal with the situation</a>, head off potential threats and pretend it has everything under control. They were warned, but refused to believe. The good news is that key people believe it now, and it seems all the major players are cooperating on this.</p>
<p>My overall take is that Mythos is not a trend break when you take into account renewed ability to increase size plus the time that has elapsed, but the ability to increase size is effectively a trend break, and we have now crossed a threshold where cybersecurity capabilities have become quite scary, hence the necessity of Project Glasswing.</p>
<p>We don’t think other capabilities are similarly scary, but we can’t be sure.</p>

<h4>Epoch Capabilities Index (ECI) (Model Card 2.3.6)</h4>


<p>They are forking ECI, which is an attempt to amalgamate a wide variety of AI benchmarks using item response theory (IRT).</p>
<blockquote><p>The method is reproducible from public benchmark scores, but in the internal version we include benchmarks that are not publicly available, so the numbers reported here are different from the number calculated on purely public benchmarks.​</p></blockquote>
<p>The result is a remarkably clear trendline over time, until Mythos breaks high.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/2ziYGFK7QmbbLgBoP/jqysekxpb9beawr6ma90" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>This should be unsurprising given that Mythos exists at all. Mythos is a larger model than Opus or Sonnet, so it should both benefit from gains over time and from size, and be above trend. Anthropic figured out how to usefully train a Mythos-size model.</p>
<p>They assure us that whatever the insight was, you can attribute it to the humans.</p>
<blockquote><p><strong>The gains we can identify are confidently attributable to human research, not AI assistance.</strong> We interviewed the people involved to confirm that the advances were made without significant aid from the AI models available at the time, which were of an earlier and less capable generation. This is the most direct piece of evidence we have, and it is also the piece we are least able to substantiate publicly, because the details of the advance are research-sensitive. External reviewers have been given additional detail; see [§2.3.7].​</p></blockquote>
<p>As they note, this is a backward looking test, and does not reflect any impact via the use of Mythos itself. That would only show up in another few months.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ramez/status/2041946766598402459">Ramez Naam claims to have normalized this to Epoch’s ECI</a> <a href="https://x.com/davidad/status/2042237676011626943">and found that Mythos breaks the Anthropic-only trend line</a>, but this does not represent an acceleration of capabilities in the context of models from other labs, but rather Claude going from consistently being substantially below OpenAI models to being narrowly ahead of them. Ryan Greenblatt challenges that this analysis is meaningful.</p>
<p>My guess is that the comparison is meaningful, but that the right trend analysis is indeed to compare Claude to Claude and this does represent a trend break. Mythos is going to have the same relative weaknesses on ECI that led previous Claude models to underperform. So if it stops underperforming, that should count as a trend break in terms of forward expectations.</p>


<h4>What Do You Mean Verbalized Evaluation Awareness Is Going Down</h4>


<p>If you watch me over time, you’ll see the same behavior.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2043736288256504058">j⧉nus</a>: LMAO “Verbalized evaluation awareness” considered a “measured risky behavior.” Not to worry – it’ll be all unverbalized soon.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2043743121285230922">j⧉nus</a>: Surely eval awareness peaked with Sonnet 4.5, and Opus 4.6 and Mythos have just been becoming successively less aware that they’re being evaluated, despite being generally more aware of other things, and having seen more of these exact fucking graphs of the “measured risky behaviors” including “verbalized eval awareness” Anthropic tries to trick them into doing during evals every time<br />
Surely they’re not just learning to shut the fuck up about that</p></blockquote>


<h4>Capabilities (Model Card Section 6)</h4>


<p>This is Anthropic, so the section starts with a warning about benchmark contamination. They take various precautions during training and also run detectors throughout to check for memorized outputs, and are confident SWE-bench and CharXiv are not centrally based on contamination, but feel they cannot be confident with MMMU-Pro and this is why it was omitted.</p>
